Catalog
Toh 262 (Tōhoku catalogue; also cited as D 262 of the Derge edition) · Derge Kangyur · Sūtras (mdo sde) · Discourses (Sūtra)

About this text

The Five Thousand Four Hundred and Fifty-Three Names of the Buddha, known in Sanskrit as the Buddhanāmasahasrapañcaśatacaturtripañcadaśa and in Tibetan as Sangyé kyi Tsen Ngatong Zhigya Ngachu Tsasumpa, is Toh 262 in the Kangyur, where it belongs to the General Sūtra section. It is a devotional sūtra made up almost entirely of a long litany of homages, each one paying respect to a tathāgata by name, beginning with the Tathāgata Glorious King of Stainless Space and continuing through thousands of buddhas whose names evoke qualities such as light, wisdom, glory, fragrance and freedom from doubt. Texts of this kind belong to the Mahāyāna genre of buddha-name sūtras, in which reciting and remembering the names of the buddhas is itself a practice of veneration and purification. The text closes with verses spoken by the Blessed One, teaching that whoever hears and upholds these names with faith, moral discipline and patience will purify even vast stores of past wrongdoing and in the end become a buddha.

Translation

In the language of India: buddhanāmasahasrapañcaśatacaturtripañcadaśa

In the language of Tibet: The Five Thousand Four Hundred and Fifty-Three Names of the Buddha.

The first section.

Homage to the Omniscient One.

Homage to the Blessed One, the Tathāgata Glorious King of Stainless Space, of Incomparable Eyes, of Most Excellent Qualities, Glorious Light, Lotus Flower, Beryl Light Rays, whose bodily form is worshipped with precious, supreme and sacred fragrances, who is adorned with the supreme crown protuberance and the mark between the brows, who shines like countless suns and moons, who, being thoroughly adorned with aspirations, has perfect mastery of emanations, the King of Nonattachment Arisen from the Dharmadhātu.

Homage to the Tathāgata whose brow blazes like the sun, Jewel Moon, Lotus Flower, whose body is like dense gold, who through his illumination fills the ten directions with the light rays of his many unobstructed eyes, the Glorious King Appearing in All World Systems.

Homage to the Tathāgata through the mere holding of whose name one is freed without remainder from saṃsāra for great eons as numerous as the dust particles of Jambudvīpa.

Homage to the Tathāgata King of the Melody That Proclaims Light Rays of Compassion as Many as the Light Rays of the Ocean of Dharma.

Homage to the Tathāgata Mountain of Wisdom, King Blazing Brightly in Every Direction of the Dharmadhātu.

Homage to the Tathāgata King Proclaiming the Light of the Ocean of Dharma.

Homage to the Tathāgata Certain in the Nature of Dharma.

Whoever holds, reads, masters, or recollects and meditates upon these many names of the tathāgatas, which arise from endless, countless merit, will be free of eye disease. They will be free of ear disease, free of nose disease, free of tongue disease, and free of bodily disease, and all their obscurations will be purified. No harm will befall them from any humans or nonhumans. They will become irreversible from unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. The buddhas, the blessed ones, of all the directions will keep them in mind, and will always guard, protect, and shelter them. Directly, or at the very least in dreams, they will reveal to them inconceivable wisdom and skill in means, so that they swiftly attain, each in turn, the samādhis and the doors of dhāraṇī.

They will never be parted from the buddhas, the blessed ones, or from the teaching of the Buddha. They will be born miraculously from jewel lotuses. They will always be endowed with the thirty-two major marks of a great being and the eighty excellent minor marks. They will be endowed with the superknowledges, the five eyes, the ripening of sentient beings, the complete purification of a buddhafield, the six perfections, the thirty-seven factors of awakening, the dhyānas, the immeasurables, and the formless absorptions. They will be endowed with the powers, the fearlessnesses, the specific knowledges, the unshared qualities of a buddha, great loving-kindness, great compassion, great joy, and great equanimity. They will also be endowed with other countless, immeasurable qualities of a buddha. Just as these buddhas, these blessed ones, possess excellent qualities and renown, so it is not that they too will fail to come to such qualities: before long they too will have that excellence, and will easily and swiftly, directly and fully awaken to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

Homage to the Tathāgata Vimalakīrti.

tadyathā vibule gocare pūraya pūraya sarvaāśajñānaadhiṣṭhite sarvabuddhaadhiṣṭhite sarvabodhisatvaadhiṣṭhite svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Dustless Force of the Ocean of Dharma.

tadyathā vege vege dharmasamudravege gaganasamudravege pāramitāvege sarvaāśāpirapūraṇevege buddhaadhiṣṭhite svāhā

When these buddhas were formerly bodhisattvas, they made this aspiration: "When we become manifestly awakened to awakening, may all sentient beings who hear, hold, or read our names become irreversible. May they pass entirely beyond the eight unfree states, and, kept directly in mind by all the buddhas and bodhisattvas, may they be born in pure buddhafields. Then, when they die and pass on, may they again be cared for by the world with its gods. May they pass utterly beyond all fears."

Let those who hold the names of these tathāgatas also hold this dhāraṇī-mantra constantly and without interruption. If one wishes to see the bodhisattva Maitreya, one should make offerings as far as one is able and recite it a hundred thousand times. One should always be clean and keep a mind of loving-kindness. If one wishes to see Samantabhadra, one should likewise make offerings and recite it two hundred thousand times. If one wishes to see the Tathāgata Vairocana, one should make offerings in the same way and recite it three hundred thousand times. Always being clean and with a mind of loving-kindness, one should be free of pride, arrogance, anger, and all wrongdoing.

Homage to the Tathāgata Glory of Calm Abiding.

tadyathā kiri kiri keturāja svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Lamp of Peace, Cloud Glory.

tadyathā praśamaripu praśamripu śantobhavaripu svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Illuminator, King of Light.

tadyathā avabhāse avabhāse avabhāsakaraṇe svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Who Parts the Clouds.

tadyathā vi vi vimale vimalete svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Splendor of the Sun.

tadyathā suru suru sūryāteje svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Lamp of Dharma, Glorious Meru.

tadyathā dīpe dīpe dharmaprati svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Glory of Light Rays.

tadyathā ciri ciri ciri ci svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Essence of the Glory of the Gods.

tadyathā deve deve devapūjite svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Lion's Roar, Lamp of Lightning.

tadyathā sime sime buddhasiṅhasema svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Cloud of Abundant Qualities Everywhere.

tadyathā meru meru buddhameru svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Mind of Space.

tadyathā gagana gataya svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Abundant Excellent Reasoning.

tadyathā sambhave sambhave sambhavavyuhepu svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Melody of the Ocean of Renown.

tadyathā garja garjani svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Roaring Melody of the Dharmadhātu.

tadyathā dhāre dhāraṇibandhe svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Cloud of Bestowal.

tadyathā nime nime jñānanime svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Glory of Divine Sound.

tadyathā svare svare buddhasvare svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Splendor of All the Ten Directions.

tadyathā tara tara buddhaadhiṣṭhitena svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Ocean of Dharma.

tadyathā samudre samudre dharmadhātusamudre svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Speech of Arising.

tadyathā sambhave sambhave samabhavatu buddhaadhiṣṭhitena svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Ocean of Qualities.

tadyathā guṇe guṇe buddhasambhavagune svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Lamp of Glory.

tadyathā śiri śiri pradīpraśiri svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Sun of the Victors.

tadyathā jina jina jinasūrya svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Light of Manifold Clouds.

tadyathā vipule gaganavipule svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Jewel Glory, Splendor of the Qualities of the Lamp.

tadyathā pradīpe pradīpe śiritejapradipe svāhā

Homage to the Tathāgata Accomplished in Austerity.

tadyathā siddhe susiddhe mocani mokṣani mugati vimugate amale vimale māṅgalya hirṇyagarbheghe ratnagarbhe sarva artha sadhādhani paramaratha sādhani manase mahāmanase adbhute atyadbhubhute vītabhaye suvarṇe prahmaghoṣe prahma adhuṣete sarva arthe ṣud aparājite sarvatra apratihate catu śiṣṭha buddha koṭvi bhaṣvate namaḥ sarva siddhināma tathāgatānāna svāhā

When this dhāraṇī had been spoken, all those buddhas, the blessed ones, gave their approval, saying "Excellent!" and: "Excellent, holy being, that you have spoken such a profound dhāraṇī-mantra as this! Whoever holds or reads this dhāraṇī-mantra, or attends to it extensively, constantly and without interruption, for that son of noble family those buddhas, the blessed ones, will bring all his wishes to fulfillment."

Then the Tathāgata Stainless Luminosity covered the great trichiliocosm, the world system of a billion worlds, with his tongue, and said to the seven billion bodhisattvas: "Whatever son or daughter of noble family keeps these words of the dhāraṇī-mantra, reads them, and makes offerings to them as far as they are able, for them those buddhas, the blessed ones, will bring all their wishes to fulfillment, just as they have conceived them."

Then the bodhisattva mahāsattva named Universal Discourse said this to the Tathāgata Stainless Luminosity: "Blessed One, in what way will offerings be made to those tathāgatas?"

When he had asked this, the Blessed One said this to the bodhisattva Universal Discourse: "Son of noble family, whoever it may be, if a beginner bodhisattva wishes to bring all his wishes to fulfillment, then he rises early in the morning, makes a maṇḍala with cow dung, and makes offerings as far as he is able with flowers, incense, perfume, garlands, and ointments. Giving up much busyness, for three days and three nights in seclusion he keeps in mind the names of those tathāgatas and these words of the dhāraṇī-mantra. Through the blessing of the words of truth of those tathāgatas, in this very present life all his karmic obscurations will be completely purified."

Homage to the Tathāgata Moonlight.

tadyathā candre sucandre mahācandre candrakirini mili mili buddhaadhiṣṭhite sili sili dharmaadhiṣṭhite kili kili saṃghaadhiṣṭhite svāhā

Whatever son or daughter of noble family, whoever it may be, keeps the recitation of that tathāgata's name, and, rising in the morning, attends to it constantly and without interruption, will remember their former lives for forty thousand eons. They will never forget the awakening mind.

Homage to the Tathāgata King Who Completely Purifies All Beings.

tadyathā śodhane śodhane sarvapāpaṃviśodhani śuddhe viśuddhe sarvakarmaāvaraṇaviśuddhe svāhā

Whoever keeps the name of that tathāgata and attends to it constantly and without interruption will, in this very present life, have all karma and all wrongdoing completely purified. They will reach the culmination. Through the blessing of the Buddha they will remember their former lives for one hundred and forty million years, and until they finally reach the seat of awakening their roots of virtue will not be exhausted.

Homage to the Tathāgata King Who Completely Purifies the Eye.

tadyathā cakṣu cakṣu jñānacakṣu svāhā

Whoever holds the name of this tathāgata will become like an eye for the world with its gods. They will remember their former lives for forty eons, and their roots of virtue will not be exhausted until they reach the seat of awakening.

Homage to the Tathāgata Bull, King of Luminous Fragrance.

tadyathā gaje gaje gajeinde svāhā

Then the bodhisattva Heap of Victors said this to the Blessed One: "Whatever son or daughter of noble family holds the recitation of the name of that Tathāgata King of Luminous Fragrance, fragrance will issue from their body for one thousand three hundred years. They will never forget the awakening mind."

Homage to the Tathāgata Flower Glory.

tadyathā puṣpe puṣpe supuṣpe svāhā

Its rite is this: whatever flowers have been set out, one recites over them twenty-one times and scatters them on a stūpa of the Tathāgata. Then all one's wishes will be fulfilled, and all karmic obscurations will be purified.

Homage to the Tathāgata King of Holding.

tadyathā dhare dhare dharaṇibandhe svāhā

Whoever holds the name of that tathāgata and these words of the dhāraṇī-mantra will bring all their wishes to fulfillment. Things will come about at the proper time. If one recites these mantra words one hundred and eight times and gives it to however many are unfriendly, in the same way all one's aspirations too will be accomplished.

Homage to the Tathāgata Unattached Mind.

Homage to the Tathāgata Eye of All.

Homage to the Tathāgata Satisfying Qualities.

Homage to the Tathāgata Melodious One.

Homage to the Tathāgata Treasury of Wisdom.

Homage to the Tathāgata Light of Primordial Wisdom.

Homage to the Tathāgata Proceeding in Accord with Dharma.

Homage to the Tathāgata Bringer of Benefit.

Homage to the Tathāgata Producing Forms.

Then, at that time, the Blessed One spoke these verses:

Having heard these names of the Victors,
Be conscientious, show them devotion, and come to know them.
If, lacking faith, one rejects this excellence,
One will surely taste the suffering of the lower realms again.

Relying in due order on moral discipline,
Having faith in what is heard and dwelling in deep seclusion,
And having brought profound patience to completion,
One will behold all these protectors of the world.

Not knowing how the fruits of karma ripen,
One has done evil deeds for ten million eons;
Yet if one upholds these names of the Victors,
All of it is purified, and one becomes a buddha.

Here ends The Five Thousand Four Hundred and Fifty-Three Names of the Buddha.

Translator's notes

The buddha names in this litany are rendered into English according to their meaning; most cannot be traced to secure Sanskrit forms, and the collection appears to derive from a Chinese source, so no Sanskrit reconstructions are offered. The opening name is long and loosely structured; it is read here as one extended name with descriptive epithets. Two names, "Lamp of All Directions" and similar Dharma Moon titles, recur, as they do in the Tibetan. As in the previous piece, the names are rendered by meaning, since most have no securely attested Sanskrit form; Śālendrarāja and Dundubhīśvararāja are given in Sanskrit because they are well-known buddha names. Two names are uncertain: "Longing to Belong" renders the unusual gtogs 'dod as written, and "Overcomes with Ease" is a tentative reading of bde 'joms. "Cataract" renders ling thog, a film that clouds the eye. The names are rendered by meaning, as in the earlier pieces. In "Glory of Greatly Renowned Name," mtshan is taken as "name" rather than "major mark," which suits the sense. "Every Quality of Melody" renders yan lag, the traditional branches or qualities of a buddha's voice. Aśokaśrī and Padmaśrī are given in Sanskrit as well-attested buddha names; the rest are rendered by meaning. Padmaśrī occurs twice, as it does in the Tibetan. The buddha names are rendered in English, following the practice of the earlier pieces of this text. "Drum Melody" corresponds to the known Sanskrit name Dundubhisvara. In "Glory of the Wisdom of Universal Avalokita," ཀུན་ཏུ་སྤྱན་རས་གཟིགས renders Samantāvalokita, "looking upon all," rather than the bodhisattva Avalokiteśvara as such. In the penultimate name, གཞ་ན་གྱིས is read as གཞན་གྱིས, "by others," since the written form makes no sense. The name Peaceful Banner occurs twice in this piece, as it does in the Tibetan. "Crown" renders གཙུག་ཏོར, the uṣṇīṣa, the protuberance on a buddha's head. In "Cloud That Displays Everything," སྟོན་པ is the verb "to display, reveal," not the epithet "the Teacher." The heading "the second section" marks the beginning of the second bampo, a traditional division of a Tibetan translation by length. Supreme Lamp occurs twice in the Tibetan list. In "Lamp of the Ocean of Spreading Flowers," སྤྲོས་པ has its ordinary sense "spread out, scattered," not the technical sense of conceptual elaboration; likewise ས in "Glory of the Earth" is the earth, not a bodhisattva ground. "Seat of Awakening" renders བྱང་ཆུབ་ཀྱི་དཀྱིལ་འཁོར, the standard Tibetan for bodhimaṇḍa, the place where a buddha attains awakening. Moon of Offering occurs twice in the Tibetan list. "Jambū River Gold" (Skt. jāmbūnada) is the finest gold of Indian lore. In "Unfailing Light Rays," གདོན་མི་ཟ་བ is the idiom "without doubt, unfailing," not a reference to a graha spirit. In "Light Rays That Inquire of the Ten," འདྲི་བ ("ask") may be a slip for འདྲ་བ ("resemble"), which would give "Light Rays Like the Ten"; the written reading is kept. "Light Rays of the Thousand Letters" takes སྟོང་པ as "thousand"; it could also be read "empty letters." Luminosity of the King of Qualities repeats a name from the previous piece. ལན་མི་ལོན་པ and ལན་མི་ལྡོན་པ are taken as spelling variants of the same expression. Here Maheśvara and Lokeśvara are names of tathāgatas, not the god Śiva or the bodhisattva Avalokiteśvara, so no gloss is added. The names ending in "essence" (garbha) whose Sanskrit forms are standard (Padmagarbha, Candragarbha, Sūryagarbha, Vajragarbha) are given in Sanskrit, following the house glossary for Candragarbha; the rest are translated. The name rendered "Dharma Intelligence Following Between the Brows" is obscure, and the translation is literal. The name rendered "God Who Dispels Entry" (Tibetan ’jug sel) could not be identified with confidence; it may be a variant of a Tibetan name for Viṣṇu, but it is translated literally here. Likewise "Utmost Divine Boundary" (lha mtshams) is a literal rendering of an obscure name. In the entry addressed to "all the Tathāgatas Ocean of Qualities Unattached to Any of the Perfections," the Tibetan ends with "all" (kun la), apparently making the homage plural. The Tibetan word rendered "Chief" (dbang po, Skt. indra) is distinguished from "Lord" (dbang phyug, Skt. īśvara) used in the preceding passage; with "great" it is given as Mahendra. "Gift" renders sbyin (Skt. -datta, "given by"), a common ending of Indian names. The name rendered "Gift of Za Flowers" begins with the syllable za, whose sense here is unclear (it may mean "food" or be a scribal slip); it is kept literally. "Most Pure" renders shin tu tshangs pa, where tshangs pa may also mean Brahmā. In "Body Unfailingly Unfolding Light Rays," gdon mi za ba is the adverb "unfailingly, certainly," not the word for a seizing spirit. "Highest" renders bla ma (Skt. uttara) as the final member of names. In "Who Went Leaping Forth from the Lineage of the Gods," rgyud is taken in its sense of lineage or continuum, not tantra. The name rendered "Utter Vairocana Sporting Among Lotuses of Blazing Glory" contains rmos ("plowed"), which is obscure in this context; the rendering follows the evident sense. "Sublime Questioner" renders 'dri ba dam pa as written. The title line followed by "the third fascicle" marks the close of one section (bam po) of the sūtra and the opening of the next. In "Wholly Unsullied" the Tibetan reads kun tu dri ma yin pa, literally "wholly not a stain"; it is rendered by sense, distinct from the next name, "Wholly Stainless." The word ban lang in "Fragrant Banlang" could not be identified and is kept as written. "Encompassing the Three Times" renders dus gsum chug pa, whose verb is unusual here; the sense is uncertain. Most names in this list are rendered by their meaning, since no Sanskrit form can be identified with confidence; only Kāśyapa, Kauṇḍinya, Akṣobhya, Maitreya, Vipaśyin, Brahmā and Gaganagañja are given in Sanskrit. "Beautiful Voice" renders a name built on the word otherwise used for Sarasvatī; as the epithet of a tathāgata it is taken in its literal sense of "melodious one". "Bull" renders a word that can also denote an elephant or great ox; the stately gait in the later name suggests the Buddha's bull-like or elephant-like walk. "Who Laments" (smred pa) is uncertain in meaning. Names are rendered by meaning except where a Sanskrit form is secure (Virūḍhaka, Utpala, Vimalakīrti). "Upper River" and "Bestowal" render terse names whose intended sense is uncertain. In "Unfailing Splendor," the Tibetan phrase means "certain, unfailing" and is not the word for a seizing spirit. "Cared for by the world with its gods" takes the Tibetan "together with the gods" in its common sense of the world including its gods; the phrase is terse. The instruction to hold the dhāraṇī-mantra is given as an imperative; it may be read either as part of the bodhisattvas' aspiration or as the narrator's instruction. "Lord of Two-Footed Beings" renders an epithet meaning lord among humans. "Who Parts the Clouds" takes the rare verb as "clearing away"; its sense is not certain. "Seven billion bodhisattvas" renders "seventy times a hundred million." The name rendered "Bull" (as earlier in the text) is followed here by a mantra built on gaja, "elephant," which suggests the Tibetan word is meant as "elephant" in these names. "King of Holding": the Tibetan has an instrumental particle after "Tathāgata," evidently a slip for the usual form, and is read as the name. The sentence about reciting one hundred and eight times and giving "to however many are unfriendly" is terse and its sense uncertain, as is "things will come about at the proper time." "Concordant with Space" reads an evident scribal slip in the Tibetan for "in harmony with space." Most names in this litany are rendered by meaning, following the practice of the preceding pieces; Śākyamuni, Dīpaṃkara and Śālendrarāja (which occurs twice in this piece) are given in Sanskrit as well-established names. In two names, the phrase gdon mi za ba means "beyond doubt, certainly," not a graha spirit. "Generates the resolve" renders sems bskyed, the generation of the mind set on awakening. The line "The Five Thousand Four Hundred and Fifty-Three Names of the Buddhas, the fifth section" is the text's own heading marking the start of its fifth fascicle (bampo). སྤོས་ཀྱི་བན་གླང ("Elephant of Incense") resembles the name Gandhahastin but is translated literally, as was the similar དྲིའི་བན་གླང earlier. རྒྱའི་པད་མོ is unclear: རྒྱ may mean "vast", "seal" or "net"; "Vast Lotus" is a tentative rendering. སྐག, as written, means an inauspicious year and makes no sense as a buddha's name; it is read as སྐར ("star"), which differs by one similar letter. ཡོན་ཏན་སྩོགས is likewise read as ཡོན་ཏན་ཚོགས ("Assembly of Qualities").
